========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, nice to meet you too, doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, I just don’t have the push I used to have. For anything, really. Like my choir... I used to never miss a Sunday. Brother Thomas leads it, and he always wears these real loud yellow ties. I told him once, I said, 'Thomas, that tie is brighter than the morning sun.' He’s a good man, though. His wife makes the best potato salad for the church picnics, she puts that sweet relish in it. I always ask her for the recipe but she won't give it up. 

But anyway... I just don't go to choir now. Or do my quilting. I was working on a nice quilt, but I ran out of the blue thread, and the sewing store downtown closed up a few years back. It used to be a bakery before that, they had these little cherry tarts. So now... most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... usually just the game shows. The commercials are so loud these days, aren't they? Selling all sorts of mops and car insurance. But I just sit there with it on low, even though I know folks are expecting me to come visit or show up to things. I just can't seem to get up and go anymore.

Depressed? Well, I don't think so. I'm not crying into my soup or anything like that. Not like my neighbor Shirley. Now she gets real depressed. She lost her little dog last spring and she just wept and wept. It was a Pomeranian, I think. Fluffy little thing, but it had a mean streak. My grandmother used to say too much rain makes the tomatoes split right on the vine, and she was right. She grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw. But no... I wouldn't say I feel sad or blue myself. I just don't have the get-up-and-go anymore.

What month? Well, it's October. I'm pretty sure it's October. I just flipped the page on my calendar in the kitchen a few days ago. The bank gives them out for free if you ask nice, the teller with the curly hair always saves me one. This year they have pictures of covered bridges. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. I was just thinking about New Year's, actually. When I was a little girl, my father would bring out the good radio for New Year's Eve. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. They just fly by so fast now.

Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Just watching the birds, mostly.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, nice to meet you too, doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, I just don’t have the push I used to have. For anything, really. Like my choir... I used to never miss a Sunday. Brother Thomas leads it, and he always wears these real loud yellow ties. I told him once, I said, 'Thomas, that tie is brighter than the morning sun.' He’s a good man, though. His wife makes the best potato salad for the church picnics, she puts that sweet relish in it. I always ask her for the recipe but she won't give it up. \n\nBut anyway... I just don't go to choir now. Or do my quilting. I was working on a nice quilt, but I ran out of the blue thread, and the sewing store downtown closed up a few years back. It used to be a bakery before that, they had these little cherry tarts. So now... most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... usually just the game shows. The commercials are so loud these days, aren't they? Selling all sorts of mops and car insurance. But I just sit there with it on low, even though I know folks are expecting me to come visit or show up to things. I just can't seem to get up and go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well, I don't think so. I'm not crying into my soup or anything like that. Not like my neighbor Shirley. Now she gets real depressed. She lost her little dog last spring and she just wept and wept. It was a Pomeranian, I think. Fluffy little thing, but it had a mean streak. It used to nip right at the mailman's ankles every single morning. The mailman, Gary, he was such a nice boy, too. He always made sure to put my magazines inside the screen door so they wouldn't get wet when it rained. It’s been raining so much lately, hasn't it? My grandmother used to say too much rain makes the tomatoes split right on the vine, and she was right. She grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw. But no... I wouldn't say I feel sad or blue myself. I just don't have the get-up-and-go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is still sharp as a tack. I remember all sorts of things just fine. Like just yesterday I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those violets for near on ten years now. My sister gave them to me in this little ceramic pot that looked like a frog. She always liked frogs... used to collect them. Glass ones, wooden ones, she even had a stuffed one that croaked when you squeezed its belly. She moved down to Florida a while back. Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Pensacola. They get terrible hurricanes down there, just awful. The wind blows the roofs right off the houses, I saw it on the news. The newsman had on a very nice raincoat, though. Bright yellow... kind of like Brother Thomas's tie, actually. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, remembering things. No, my memory is perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: What month is it right now?\n\nPatient: What month? Well, it's October. I'm pretty sure it's October. I just flipped the page on my calendar in the kitchen a few days ago. The bank gives them out for free if you ask nice, the teller with the curly hair always saves me one. This year they have pictures of covered bridges. I always liked covered bridges... my uncle Henry lived out near one in Pennsylvania. Or maybe it was Ohio. He raised pigs out there. Big, muddy things, they smell just awful in the summer heat. But he made his own sausage, and let me tell you, you haven't lived until you've had Uncle Henry's breakfast sausage with a little maple syrup poured over it. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. I was just thinking about New Year's, actually. When I was a little girl, my father would bring out the good radio for New Year's Eve. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. They just fly by so fast now.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Just watching the birds, mostly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you tell me where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Just watching the birds, mostly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Getting lost? Oh, no, I don't think I've gotten lost. I know my way around the neighborhood well enough. I've lived in the same house for... oh, it must be over forty years now. It's the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree drops so many leaves in the autumn, it's a real chore to rake them all up. My grandson used to come over and help me rake them into big piles, and then he'd jump right in! He was such a rambunctious little boy. Always had scraped knees from climbing things he shouldn't. I used to keep those character band-aids in the bathroom just for him. The ones with the little cartoon dogs on them. He wouldn't let me put a plain one on, no sir. It had to be the cartoon dogs. They don't make band-aids like they used to, though. They just don't stick right if you get them even a little bit wet. I was washing dishes the other day—just a few plates, I don't use the dishwasher much anymore since it's just me—and the band-aid came right off in the soapy water. It was a lemon-scented dish soap. I always buy the lemon kind, it smells so clean. But anyway... no, I haven't been getting lost.

I used to never miss a Sunday. Brother Thomas leads it, and he always wears these real loud yellow ties. I told him once, I said, 'Thomas, that tie is brighter than the morning sun.' He’s a good man, though. His wife makes the best potato salad for the church picnics, she puts that sweet relish in it. I always ask her for the recipe but she won't give it up. \n\nBut anyway... I just don't go to choir now. Or do my quilting. I was working on a nice quilt, but I ran out of the blue thread, and the sewing store downtown closed up a few years back. The commercials are so loud these days, aren't they? Selling all sorts of mops and car insurance. I'm not crying into my soup or anything like that. Not like my neighbor Shirley. Now she gets real depressed. She lost her little dog last spring and she just wept and wept. It was a Pomeranian, I think. Fluffy little thing, but it had a mean streak. It used to nip right at the mailman's ankles every single morning. The mailman, Gary, he was such a nice boy, too. He always made sure to put my magazines inside the screen door so they wouldn't get wet when it rained. It’s been raining so much lately, hasn't it? My grandmother used to say too much rain makes the tomatoes split right on the vine, and she was right. She grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw. But no... I wouldn't say I feel sad or blue myself. I just don't have the get-up-and-go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is still sharp as a tack. I remember all sorts of things just fine. Like just yesterday I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those violets for near on ten years now. My sister gave them to me in this little ceramic pot that looked like a frog. She always liked frogs... used to collect them. Glass ones, wooden ones, she even had a stuffed one that croaked when you squeezed its belly. She moved down to Florida a while back. Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Pensacola. I just flipped the page on my calendar in the kitchen a few days ago. The bank gives them out for free if you ask nice, the teller with the curly hair always saves me one. This year they have pictures of covered bridges. I always liked covered bridges... my uncle Henry lived out near one in Pennsylvania. Or maybe it was Ohio. He raised pigs out there. Big, muddy things, they smell just awful in the summer heat. But he made his own sausage, and let me tell you, you haven't lived until you've had Uncle Henry's breakfast sausage with a little maple syrup poured over it. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. I was just thinking about New Year's, actually. When I was a little girl, my father would bring out the good radio for New Year's Eve. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. Manage my medicines? Oh, well... I don't think I need anyone to do that for me. I have them all lined up on the kitchen counter, right next to the toaster. It's one of those shiny chrome toasters, the kind that pops up with a real loud clank. My sister gave it to me for Christmas a few years back. She always wraps her presents so beautifully, with those big velvet bows that she ties herself. She learned how to make them at a craft store down in Florida when she lived there. Florida is so humid, though, I could never stand the heat. My hair just frizzes up into an absolute mess the minute I step off the plane. I used to use those big pink curlers to try and tame it, the ones you sleep in? Goodness, they were uncomfortable. Like sleeping on a pile of rocks. But yes, I just take my pills myself when I have my morning tea.

Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. It was a Pomeranian, I think. Fluffy little thing, but it had a mean streak. It used to nip right at the mailman's ankles every single morning. The mailman, Gary, he was such a nice boy, too. My grandmother used to say too much rain makes the tomatoes split right on the vine, and she was right. She grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw. But no... I wouldn't say I feel sad or blue myself. I just don't have the get-up-and-go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is still sharp as a tack. I remember all sorts of things just fine. Like just yesterday I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those violets for near on ten years now. My sister gave them to me in this little ceramic pot that looked like a frog. She always liked frogs... used to collect them. Glass ones, wooden ones, she even had a stuffed one that croaked when you squeezed its belly. She moved down to Florida a while back. Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Pensacola. They get terrible hurricanes down there, just awful. The wind blows the roofs right off the houses, I saw it on the news. The newsman had on a very nice raincoat, though. Bright yellow... kind of like Brother Thomas's tie, actually. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, remembering things. No, my memory is perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: What month is it right now?\n\nPatient: What month? Well, it's October. I'm pretty sure it's October. I just flipped the page on my calendar in the kitchen a few days ago. The bank gives them out for free if you ask nice, the teller with the curly hair always saves me one. This year they have pictures of covered bridges. I always liked covered bridges... my uncle Henry lived out near one in Pennsylvania. Or maybe it was Ohio. He raised pigs out there. Big, muddy things, they smell just awful in the summer heat. But he made his own sausage, and let me tell you, you haven't lived until you've had Uncle Henry's breakfast sausage with a little maple syrup poured over it. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. I was just thinking about New Year's, actually. When I was a little girl, my father would bring out the good radio for New Year's Eve. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. They just fly by so fast now.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Just watching the birds, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? Oh, no, I don't think I've gotten lost. I know my way around the neighborhood well enough. I've lived in the same house for... oh, it must be over forty years now. It's the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree drops so many leaves in the autumn, it's a real chore to rake them all up. My grandson used to come over and help me rake them into big piles, and then he'd jump right in! He was such a rambunctious little boy. Always had scraped knees from climbing things he shouldn't. I used to keep those character band-aids in the bathroom just for him. The ones with the little cartoon dogs on them. He wouldn't let me put a plain one on, no sir. It had to be the cartoon dogs. They don't make band-aids like they used to, though. They just don't stick right if you get them even a little bit wet. I was washing dishes the other day—just a few plates, I don't use the dishwasher much anymore since it's just me—and the band-aid came right off in the soapy water. It was a lemon-scented dish soap. I always buy the lemon kind, it smells so clean. But anyway... no, I haven't been getting lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you need someone else to manage your medicines for you?\n\nPatient: Manage my medicines? Oh, well... I don't think I need anyone to do that for me. I have them all lined up on the kitchen counter, right next to the toaster. It's one of those shiny chrome toasters, the kind that pops up with a real loud clank. My sister gave it to me for Christmas a few years back. She always wraps her presents so beautifully, with those big velvet bows that she ties herself. She learned how to make them at a craft store down in Florida when she lived there. Florida is so humid, though, I could never stand the heat. My hair just frizzes up into an absolute mess the minute I step off the plane. I used to use those big pink curlers to try and tame it, the ones you sleep in? Goodness, they were uncomfortable. Like sleeping on a pile of rocks. But yes, I just take my pills myself when I have my morning tea.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Wrong with my thinking? No, no, I don't think so. My memory is just fine. I was just remembering the other day about the time my cousin came to visit from upstate. She brought this huge basket of apples from an orchard near her house. Honeycrisp, I think they were. They make the best pies, they really do. You have to peel them just right, though, or the texture gets all mushy when you bake it. I have this little paring knife with a wooden handle that I've used for years, fits right in the palm of my hand. The blade is getting a bit dull, I suppose, but I don't know how to sharpen it. My husband used to do all the sharpening out in the garage on his grinding wheel. Sparks would just fly everywhere! It always made me nervous he was going to catch his shirt on fire. He wore those thick flannel shirts all winter long, the plaid ones. Red and black, mostly. They were so warm, but they took forever to dry on the line. I always loved the smell of clothes dried outside in the fresh air, though... much better than the dryer. But anyway, no, my memory is just fine.

And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Just watching the birds, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? Oh, no, I don't think I've gotten lost. I know my way around the neighborhood well enough. I've lived in the same house for... oh, it must be over forty years now. It's the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree drops so many leaves in the autumn, it's a real chore to rake them all up. My grandson used to come over and help me rake them into big piles, and then he'd jump right in! He was such a rambunctious little boy. Always had scraped knees from climbing things he shouldn't. I used to keep those character band-aids in the bathroom just for him. The ones with the little cartoon dogs on them. He wouldn't let me put a plain one on, no sir. It had to be the cartoon dogs. They don't make band-aids like they used to, though. They just don't stick right if you get them even a little bit wet. I was washing dishes the other day—just a few plates, I don't use the dishwasher much anymore since it's just me—and the band-aid came right off in the soapy water. It was a lemon-scented dish soap. I always buy the lemon kind, it smells so clean. But anyway... no, I haven't been getting lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you need someone else to manage your medicines for you?\n\nPatient: Manage my medicines? Oh, well... I don't think I need anyone to do that for me. I have them all lined up on the kitchen counter, right next to the toaster. It's one of those shiny chrome toasters, the kind that pops up with a real loud clank. My sister gave it to me for Christmas a few years back. She always wraps her presents so beautifully, with those big velvet bows that she ties herself. She learned how to make them at a craft store down in Florida when she lived there. Florida is so humid, though, I could never stand the heat. My hair just frizzes up into an absolute mess the minute I step off the plane. I used to use those big pink curlers to try and tame it, the ones you sleep in? Goodness, they were uncomfortable. Like sleeping on a pile of rocks. But yes, I just take my pills myself when I have my morning tea.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything is wrong with your thinking or memory?\n\nPatient: Wrong with my thinking? No, no, I don't think so. My memory is just fine. I was just remembering the other day about the time my cousin came to visit from upstate. She brought this huge basket of apples from an orchard near her house. Honeycrisp, I think they were. They make the best pies, they really do. You have to peel them just right, though, or the texture gets all mushy when you bake it. I have this little paring knife with a wooden handle that I've used for years, fits right in the palm of my hand. The blade is getting a bit dull, I suppose, but I don't know how to sharpen it. My husband used to do all the sharpening out in the garage on his grinding wheel. Sparks would just fly everywhere! It always made me nervous he was going to catch his shirt on fire. He wore those thick flannel shirts all winter long, the plaid ones. Red and black, mostly. They were so warm, but they took forever to dry on the line. I always loved the smell of clothes dried outside in the fresh air, though... much better than the dryer. But anyway, no, my memory is just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Honeycrisp, I think they were. They make the best pies, they really do. You have to peel them just right, though, or the texture gets all mushy when you bake it. I have this little paring knife with a wooden handle that I've used for years, fits right in the palm of my hand. The blade is getting a bit dull, I suppose, but I don't know how to sharpen it. My husband used to do all the sharpening out in the garage on his grinding wheel. Sparks would just fly everywhere! It always made me nervous he was going to catch his shirt on fire. He wore those thick flannel shirts all winter long, the plaid ones. Red and black, mostly. They were so warm, but they took forever to dry on the line. I always loved the smell of clothes dried outside in the fresh air, though... much better than the dryer. But anyway, no, my memory is just fine.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having more trouble thinking or remembering than you used to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More trouble? Oh, I wouldn't say that. I think I remember things just as well as I ever did. Like yesterday, I remembered to put the trash out right on time. The garbage truck always comes so early on Tuesdays, before the sun is even fully up. It makes such an awful racket, clanging those big metal bins against the side of the truck. I don't know how the driver stands the noise all day long. He must wear earplugs, don't you think? Those little foam ones you roll up between your fingers. I tried wearing those once when my neighbor got a new dog that barked all night. It was a little terrier mix, cute as a button but just wouldn't stop yapping at every shadow. I think it was afraid of the dark. Some dogs are like that, you know. They need a little nightlight just like children do. I always kept a nightlight in the hallway when my kids were small. It was shaped like a little seashell, plugged right into the wall. Gave off the softest pink glow... But no, I don't think I'm having any more trouble than usual.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF03.90, F06.7, F32.9, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, nice to meet you too, doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, I just don’t have the push I used to have. For anything, really. Like my choir... I used to never miss a Sunday. Brother Thomas leads it, and he always wears these real loud yellow ties. I told him once, I said, 'Thomas, that tie is brighter than the morning sun.' He’s a good man, though. His wife makes the best potato salad for the church picnics, she puts that sweet relish in it. I always ask her for the recipe but she won't give it up. \n\nBut anyway... I just don't go to choir now. Or do my quilting. I was working on a nice quilt, but I ran out of the blue thread, and the sewing store downtown closed up a few years back. It used to be a bakery before that, they had these little cherry tarts. So now... most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... usually just the game shows. The commercials are so loud these days, aren't they? Selling all sorts of mops and car insurance. But I just sit there with it on low, even though I know folks are expecting me to come visit or show up to things. I just can't seem to get up and go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well, I don't think so. I'm not crying into my soup or anything like that. Not like my neighbor Shirley. Now she gets real depressed. She lost her little dog last spring and she just wept and wept. It was a Pomeranian, I think. Fluffy little thing, but it had a mean streak. It used to nip right at the mailman's ankles every single morning. The mailman, Gary, he was such a nice boy, too. He always made sure to put my magazines inside the screen door so they wouldn't get wet when it rained. It’s been raining so much lately, hasn't it? My grandmother used to say too much rain makes the tomatoes split right on the vine, and she was right. She grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw. But no... I wouldn't say I feel sad or blue myself. I just don't have the get-up-and-go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is still sharp as a tack. I remember all sorts of things just fine. Like just yesterday I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those violets for near on ten years now. My sister gave them to me in this little ceramic pot that looked like a frog. She always liked frogs... used to collect them. Glass ones, wooden ones, she even had a stuffed one that croaked when you squeezed its belly. She moved down to Florida a while back. Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Pensacola. They get terrible hurricanes down there, just awful. The wind blows the roofs right off the houses, I saw it on the news. The newsman had on a very nice raincoat, though. Bright yellow... kind of like Brother Thomas's tie, actually. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, remembering things. No, my memory is perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: What month is it right now?\n\nPatient: What month? Well, it's October. I'm pretty sure it's October. I just flipped the page on my calendar in the kitchen a few days ago. The bank gives them out for free if you ask nice, the teller with the curly hair always saves me one. This year they have pictures of covered bridges. I always liked covered bridges... my uncle Henry lived out near one in Pennsylvania. Or maybe it was Ohio. He raised pigs out there. Big, muddy things, they smell just awful in the summer heat. But he made his own sausage, and let me tell you, you haven't lived until you've had Uncle Henry's breakfast sausage with a little maple syrup poured over it. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. I was just thinking about New Year's, actually. When I was a little girl, my father would bring out the good radio for New Year's Eve. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. They just fly by so fast now.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Just watching the birds, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? Oh, no, I don't think I've gotten lost. I know my way around the neighborhood well enough. I've lived in the same house for... oh, it must be over forty years now. It's the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree drops so many leaves in the autumn, it's a real chore to rake them all up. My grandson used to come over and help me rake them into big piles, and then he'd jump right in! He was such a rambunctious little boy. Always had scraped knees from climbing things he shouldn't. I used to keep those character band-aids in the bathroom just for him. The ones with the little cartoon dogs on them. He wouldn't let me put a plain one on, no sir. It had to be the cartoon dogs. They don't make band-aids like they used to, though. They just don't stick right if you get them even a little bit wet. I was washing dishes the other day—just a few plates, I don't use the dishwasher much anymore since it's just me—and the band-aid came right off in the soapy water. It was a lemon-scented dish soap. I always buy the lemon kind, it smells so clean. But anyway... no, I haven't been getting lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you need someone else to manage your medicines for you?\n\nPatient: Manage my medicines? Oh, well... I don't think I need anyone to do that for me. I have them all lined up on the kitchen counter, right next to the toaster. It's one of those shiny chrome toasters, the kind that pops up with a real loud clank. My sister gave it to me for Christmas a few years back. She always wraps her presents so beautifully, with those big velvet bows that she ties herself. She learned how to make them at a craft store down in Florida when she lived there. Florida is so humid, though, I could never stand the heat. My hair just frizzes up into an absolute mess the minute I step off the plane. I used to use those big pink curlers to try and tame it, the ones you sleep in? Goodness, they were uncomfortable. Like sleeping on a pile of rocks. But yes, I just take my pills myself when I have my morning tea.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything is wrong with your thinking or memory?\n\nPatient: Wrong with my thinking? No, no, I don't think so. My memory is just fine. I was just remembering the other day about the time my cousin came to visit from upstate. She brought this huge basket of apples from an orchard near her house. Honeycrisp, I think they were. They make the best pies, they really do. You have to peel them just right, though, or the texture gets all mushy when you bake it. I have this little paring knife with a wooden handle that I've used for years, fits right in the palm of my hand. The blade is getting a bit dull, I suppose, but I don't know how to sharpen it. My husband used to do all the sharpening out in the garage on his grinding wheel. Sparks would just fly everywhere! It always made me nervous he was going to catch his shirt on fire. He wore those thick flannel shirts all winter long, the plaid ones. Red and black, mostly. They were so warm, but they took forever to dry on the line. I always loved the smell of clothes dried outside in the fresh air, though... much better than the dryer. But anyway, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having more trouble thinking or remembering than you used to?\n\nPatient: More trouble? Oh, I wouldn't say that. It was shaped like a little seashell, plugged right into the wall. Gave off the softest pink glow... But no, I don't think I'm having any more trouble than usual.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. A medical condition for my brain? No, no doctor has ever said anything like that to me. I haven't been to the doctor much lately anyway, except when I went to see Dr. Evans for my bad knee a while back. He has this huge aquarium in his waiting room, right against the wall. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Finances? Oh, well, I suppose I haven't been keeping track of things quite the same way. The ladies from church have been checking on me a lot more lately, making sure I have what I need. I actually had to step back from leading the choir. And the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I promised to do. It’s a shame, really, because we were working on this beautiful quilt for the charity auction. All these little squares of fabric, donated by people all over town. Mrs. Higgins brought in a whole bag of old calico dresses she didn't wear anymore. They had the sweetest little floral prints on them, tiny pink and yellow daisies. I always loved calico, it reminds me of the aprons my grandmother used to wear when she baked bread. She’d get flour all over the front of it, just covered in white dust. Her bread was the best, though. So crusty on the outside and soft in the middle. We'd eat it warm with butter and that homemade strawberry jam she kept in the cellar. The cellar was always so cool and damp, smelled like earth and old potatoes... but anyway, yes, I've had to stop helping out as much because I just can't keep my promises straight.
