  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF03.90, F06.7, F32.9, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, nice to meet you too, doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, I just don’t have the push I used to have. For anything, really. Like my choir... I used to never miss a Sunday. Brother Thomas leads it, and he always wears these real loud yellow ties. I told him once, I said, 'Thomas, that tie is brighter than the morning sun.' He’s a good man, though. His wife makes the best potato salad for the church picnics, she puts that sweet relish in it. I always ask her for the recipe but she won't give it up. \n\nBut anyway... I just don't go to choir now. Or do my quilting. I was working on a nice quilt, but I ran out of the blue thread, and the sewing store downtown closed up a few years back. It used to be a bakery before that, they had these little cherry tarts. So now... most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... usually just the game shows. The commercials are so loud these days, aren't they? Selling all sorts of mops and car insurance. But I just sit there with it on low, even though I know folks are expecting me to come visit or show up to things. I just can't seem to get up and go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well, I don't think so. I'm not crying into my soup or anything like that. Not like my neighbor Shirley. Now she gets real depressed. She lost her little dog last spring and she just wept and wept. It was a Pomeranian, I think. Fluffy little thing, but it had a mean streak. It used to nip right at the mailman's ankles every single morning. The mailman, Gary, he was such a nice boy, too. He always made sure to put my magazines inside the screen door so they wouldn't get wet when it rained. It’s been raining so much lately, hasn't it? My grandmother used to say too much rain makes the tomatoes split right on the vine, and she was right. She grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw. But no... I wouldn't say I feel sad or blue myself. I just don't have the get-up-and-go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is still sharp as a tack. I remember all sorts of things just fine. Like just yesterday I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those violets for near on ten years now. My sister gave them to me in this little ceramic pot that looked like a frog. She always liked frogs... used to collect them. Glass ones, wooden ones, she even had a stuffed one that croaked when you squeezed its belly. She moved down to Florida a while back. Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Pensacola. They get terrible hurricanes down there, just awful. The wind blows the roofs right off the houses, I saw it on the news. The newsman had on a very nice raincoat, though. Bright yellow... kind of like Brother Thomas's tie, actually. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, remembering things. No, my memory is perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: What month is it right now?\n\nPatient: What month? Well, it's October. I'm pretty sure it's October. I just flipped the page on my calendar in the kitchen a few days ago. The bank gives them out for free if you ask nice, the teller with the curly hair always saves me one. This year they have pictures of covered bridges. I always liked covered bridges... my uncle Henry lived out near one in Pennsylvania. Or maybe it was Ohio. He raised pigs out there. Big, muddy things, they smell just awful in the summer heat. But he made his own sausage, and let me tell you, you haven't lived until you've had Uncle Henry's breakfast sausage with a little maple syrup poured over it. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. I was just thinking about New Year's, actually. When I was a little girl, my father would bring out the good radio for New Year's Eve. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. They just fly by so fast now.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Just watching the birds, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? Oh, no, I don't think I've gotten lost. I know my way around the neighborhood well enough. I've lived in the same house for... oh, it must be over forty years now. It's the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree drops so many leaves in the autumn, it's a real chore to rake them all up. My grandson used to come over and help me rake them into big piles, and then he'd jump right in! He was such a rambunctious little boy. Always had scraped knees from climbing things he shouldn't. I used to keep those character band-aids in the bathroom just for him. The ones with the little cartoon dogs on them. He wouldn't let me put a plain one on, no sir. It had to be the cartoon dogs. They don't make band-aids like they used to, though. They just don't stick right if you get them even a little bit wet. I was washing dishes the other day—just a few plates, I don't use the dishwasher much anymore since it's just me—and the band-aid came right off in the soapy water. It was a lemon-scented dish soap. I always buy the lemon kind, it smells so clean. But anyway... no, I haven't been getting lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you need someone else to manage your medicines for you?\n\nPatient: Manage my medicines? Oh, well... I don't think I need anyone to do that for me. I have them all lined up on the kitchen counter, right next to the toaster. It's one of those shiny chrome toasters, the kind that pops up with a real loud clank. My sister gave it to me for Christmas a few years back. She always wraps her presents so beautifully, with those big velvet bows that she ties herself. She learned how to make them at a craft store down in Florida when she lived there. Florida is so humid, though, I could never stand the heat. My hair just frizzes up into an absolute mess the minute I step off the plane. I used to use those big pink curlers to try and tame it, the ones you sleep in? Goodness, they were uncomfortable. Like sleeping on a pile of rocks. But yes, I just take my pills myself when I have my morning tea.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything is wrong with your thinking or memory?\n\nPatient: Wrong with my thinking? No, no, I don't think so. My memory is just fine. I was just remembering the other day about the time my cousin came to visit from upstate. She brought this huge basket of apples from an orchard near her house. Honeycrisp, I think they were. They make the best pies, they really do. You have to peel them just right, though, or the texture gets all mushy when you bake it. I have this little paring knife with a wooden handle that I've used for years, fits right in the palm of my hand. The blade is getting a bit dull, I suppose, but I don't know how to sharpen it. My husband used to do all the sharpening out in the garage on his grinding wheel. Sparks would just fly everywhere! It always made me nervous he was going to catch his shirt on fire. He wore those thick flannel shirts all winter long, the plaid ones. Red and black, mostly. They were so warm, but they took forever to dry on the line. I always loved the smell of clothes dried outside in the fresh air, though... much better than the dryer. But anyway, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having more trouble thinking or remembering than you used to?\n\nPatient: More trouble? Oh, I wouldn't say that. I think I remember things just as well as I ever did. Like yesterday, I remembered to put the trash out right on time. The garbage truck always comes so early on Tuesdays, before the sun is even fully up. It makes such an awful racket, clanging those big metal bins against the side of the truck. I don't know how the driver stands the noise all day long. He must wear earplugs, don't you think? Those little foam ones you roll up between your fingers. I tried wearing those once when my neighbor got a new dog that barked all night. It was a little terrier mix, cute as a button but just wouldn't stop yapping at every shadow. I think it was afraid of the dark. Some dogs are like that, you know. They need a little nightlight just like children do. I always kept a nightlight in the hallway when my kids were small. It was shaped like a little seashell, plugged right into the wall. Gave off the softest pink glow... But no, I don't think I'm having any more trouble than usual.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a medical condition affecting your brain or memory?\n\nPatient: A medical condition for my brain? No, no doctor has ever said anything like that to me. I haven't been to the doctor much lately anyway, except when I went to see Dr. Evans for my bad knee a while back. He has this huge aquarium in his waiting room, right against the wall. It’s full of those little blue and red fish... neon tetras, I think they’re called. They just dart around so fast, back and forth, back and forth. I always wonder who cleans that tank. It must take hours to scrub all that green algae off the glass and clean the little pebbles at the bottom. I had a goldfish bowl once when the kids were little, just a small round glass one we won at the county fair. We named him Barnaby. He lived for a really long time for a fair fish, almost two years! We buried him out by the rosebushes when he finally passed on. Those rosebushes grew so tall that next spring, with these beautiful yellow blooms. They smelled just like lemon drops, so sweet and bright. But no, nobody's told me I have any kind of memory condition.\n\nDoctor: Have you started needing help with your finances?\n\nPatient: Finances? Oh, well, I suppose I haven't been keeping track of things quite the same way. The ladies from church have been checking on me a lot more lately, making sure I have what I need. I actually had to step back from leading the choir. And the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I promised to do. It’s a shame, really, because we were working on this beautiful quilt for the charity auction. All these little squares of fabric, donated by people all over town. Mrs. Higgins brought in a whole bag of old calico dresses she didn't wear anymore. They had the sweetest little floral prints on them, tiny pink and yellow daisies. I always loved calico, it reminds me of the aprons my grandmother used to wear when she baked bread. She’d get flour all over the front of it, just covered in white dust. Her bread was the best, though. So crusty on the outside and soft in the middle. We'd eat it warm with butter and that homemade strawberry jam she kept in the cellar. The cellar was always so cool and damp, smelled like earth and old potatoes... but anyway, yes, I've had to stop helping out as much because I just can't keep my promises straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well... no, I don't think I need help with that. I still write the checks out myself every month. I sit down at the kitchen table with my special blue pen. It's one of those gel pens, you know? It writes so smooth, just glides right across the paper. My granddaughter gave it to me for my birthday last year. She’s such a thoughtful girl, always finding little things she thinks I'll like. She just started her first year of high school, can you believe it? Time goes by so fast. It feels like just yesterday she was running around the yard catching fireflies in a glass jar. We used to poke holes in the lid so they could breathe, and she’d keep them on her nightstand until they stopped glowing. Then we'd let them go in the morning. The dew would still be on the grass, getting our slippers all wet. I always wore those fuzzy pink slippers, the ones with the little pom-poms on the toes... but no, I manage my bills just fine.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt sad or low most days?\n\nPatient: Sad or low? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, everybody has their days, but I haven't been feeling down like that. Mostly I just sit on the porch in the afternoons and watch the neighborhood. The Miller boy across the street got a new bicycle, one of those bright red ones with the shiny chrome handlebars. He rides it up and down the sidewalk, ringing the little bell. It reminds me of the bell on the ice cream truck when I was a little girl. We used to hear it from three streets over and go running inside to beg my mother for a nickel. A nickel could get you a whole twin popsicle back then! We’d split it down the middle, me and my brother. He always wanted the cherry side, and I liked the orange. Orange always tasted like sunshine, you know? Like those hot July days when the tar on the road would get all soft and stick to the bottom of your bare feet. My mother would scold us for tracking black marks onto her clean linoleum floor. She kept that floor so spotless you could eat off it... but no, I haven't been feeling sad or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been missing any bill payments lately?\n\nPatient: Missing payments? Oh, well... no, I haven't missed any. I always make sure they go out on time. I walk them down to the blue mailbox on the corner every Tuesday morning. It's a nice little walk, past the Henderson's house. They just planted the most beautiful hydrangeas along their fence. Huge, blue blooms, like giant cotton candy but blue, you know? They remind me of a dress I had when I was a young woman. I wore it to a dance at the VFW hall, it had this full skirt that would just twirl and twirl when you spun around. The band was playing Glenn Miller that night, and the trumpet player had the shiniest shoes I'd ever seen. You could see the lights reflecting right off the toes of them. They must have polished them for hours. My husband used to polish his shoes like that every Sunday before church. He'd lay out a newspaper on the floor so he wouldn't get polish on the rug... but yes, my bills always get mailed right on time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It was shaped like a little seashell, plugged right into the wall. Gave off the softest pink glow... But no, I don't think I'm having any more trouble than usual.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a medical condition affecting your brain or memory?\n\nPatient: A medical condition for my brain? No, no doctor has ever said anything like that to me. I haven't been to the doctor much lately anyway, except when I went to see Dr. Evans for my bad knee a while back. He has this huge aquarium in his waiting room, right against the wall. It’s full of those little blue and red fish... neon tetras, I think they’re called. They just dart around so fast, back and forth, back and forth. I always wonder who cleans that tank. It must take hours to scrub all that green algae off the glass and clean the little pebbles at the bottom. I had a goldfish bowl once when the kids were little, just a small round glass one we won at the county fair. We named him Barnaby. He lived for a really long time for a fair fish, almost two years! We buried him out by the rosebushes when he finally passed on. Those rosebushes grew so tall that next spring, with these beautiful yellow blooms. They smelled just like lemon drops, so sweet and bright. But no, nobody's told me I have any kind of memory condition.\n\nDoctor: Have you started needing help with your finances?\n\nPatient: Finances? Oh, well, I suppose I haven't been keeping track of things quite the same way. The ladies from church have been checking on me a lot more lately, making sure I have what I need. I actually had to step back from leading the choir. And the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I promised to do. It’s a shame, really, because we were working on this beautiful quilt for the charity auction. All these little squares of fabric, donated by people all over town. Mrs. Higgins brought in a whole bag of old calico dresses she didn't wear anymore. They had the sweetest little floral prints on them, tiny pink and yellow daisies. I always loved calico, it reminds me of the aprons my grandmother used to wear when she baked bread. She’d get flour all over the front of it, just covered in white dust. Her bread was the best, though. So crusty on the outside and soft in the middle. We'd eat it warm with butter and that homemade strawberry jam she kept in the cellar. The cellar was always so cool and damp, smelled like earth and old potatoes... but anyway, yes, I've had to stop helping out as much because I just can't keep my promises straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well... no, I don't think I need help with that. I still write the checks out myself every month. I sit down at the kitchen table with my special blue pen. It's one of those gel pens, you know? It writes so smooth, just glides right across the paper. My granddaughter gave it to me for my birthday last year. She’s such a thoughtful girl, always finding little things she thinks I'll like. She just started her first year of high school, can you believe it? Time goes by so fast. It feels like just yesterday she was running around the yard catching fireflies in a glass jar. We used to poke holes in the lid so they could breathe, and she’d keep them on her nightstand until they stopped glowing. Then we'd let them go in the morning. The dew would still be on the grass, getting our slippers all wet. I always wore those fuzzy pink slippers, the ones with the little pom-poms on the toes... but no, I manage my bills just fine.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt sad or low most days?\n\nPatient: Sad or low? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, everybody has their days, but I haven't been feeling down like that. Mostly I just sit on the porch in the afternoons and watch the neighborhood. The Miller boy across the street got a new bicycle, one of those bright red ones with the shiny chrome handlebars. He rides it up and down the sidewalk, ringing the little bell. It reminds me of the bell on the ice cream truck when I was a little girl. We used to hear it from three streets over and go running inside to beg my mother for a nickel. A nickel could get you a whole twin popsicle back then! We’d split it down the middle, me and my brother. He always wanted the cherry side, and I liked the orange. Orange always tasted like sunshine, you know? Like those hot July days when the tar on the road would get all soft and stick to the bottom of your bare feet. My mother would scold us for tracking black marks onto her clean linoleum floor. She kept that floor so spotless you could eat off it... but no, I haven't been feeling sad or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been missing any bill payments lately?\n\nPatient: Missing payments? Oh, well... no, I haven't missed any. I always make sure they go out on time. I walk them down to the blue mailbox on the corner every Tuesday morning. It's a nice little walk, past the Henderson's house. They just planted the most beautiful hydrangeas along their fence. Huge, blue blooms, like giant cotton candy but blue, you know? They remind me of a dress I had when I was a young woman. I wore it to a dance at the VFW hall, it had this full skirt that would just twirl and twirl when you spun around. The band was playing Glenn Miller that night, and the trumpet player had the shiniest shoes I'd ever seen. You could see the lights reflecting right off the toes of them. They must have polished them for hours. My husband used to polish his shoes like that every Sunday before church. He'd lay out a newspaper on the floor so he wouldn't get polish on the rug... but yes, my bills always get mailed right on time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Cooking meals? Oh, no... I don't need any help with that. I still do all my own cooking. Just the other day I made a big pot of beef stew. I let it simmer on the stove for hours, and the whole kitchen smelled like carrots and thyme. I always use fresh carrots, the ones with the green leafy tops still on them. They remind me of the garden my grandfather used to keep behind his house. He grew tomatoes as big as softballs, I swear. We used to eat them right off the vine, still warm from the sun, with just a little sprinkle of salt from a glass shaker. He kept that salt shaker right in the front pocket of his overalls. Those overalls were so faded, almost white at the knees from where he'd kneel in the dirt all day. He had this old hound dog named Buster that would follow him up and down the rows, just sniffing at the dirt and trying to catch the big fat bumblebees in his mouth... but no, I manage the cooking all by myself just fine.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF03.90, F06.7, F32.9, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, nice to meet you too, doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, I just don’t have the push I used to have. For anything, really. Like my choir... I used to never miss a Sunday. Brother Thomas leads it, and he always wears these real loud yellow ties. I told him once, I said, 'Thomas, that tie is brighter than the morning sun.' He’s a good man, though. His wife makes the best potato salad for the church picnics, she puts that sweet relish in it. I always ask her for the recipe but she won't give it up. \n\nBut anyway... I just don't go to choir now. Or do my quilting. I was working on a nice quilt, but I ran out of the blue thread, and the sewing store downtown closed up a few years back. It used to be a bakery before that, they had these little cherry tarts. So now... most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... usually just the game shows. The commercials are so loud these days, aren't they? Selling all sorts of mops and car insurance. But I just sit there with it on low, even though I know folks are expecting me to come visit or show up to things. I just can't seem to get up and go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well, I don't think so. I'm not crying into my soup or anything like that. Not like my neighbor Shirley. Now she gets real depressed. She lost her little dog last spring and she just wept and wept. It was a Pomeranian, I think. Fluffy little thing, but it had a mean streak. It used to nip right at the mailman's ankles every single morning. The mailman, Gary, he was such a nice boy, too. He always made sure to put my magazines inside the screen door so they wouldn't get wet when it rained. It’s been raining so much lately, hasn't it? My grandmother used to say too much rain makes the tomatoes split right on the vine, and she was right. She grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw. But no... I wouldn't say I feel sad or blue myself. I just don't have the get-up-and-go anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Oh, no, I don't think so. My memory is still sharp as a tack. I remember all sorts of things just fine. Like just yesterday I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those violets for near on ten years now. My sister gave them to me in this little ceramic pot that looked like a frog. She always liked frogs... used to collect them. Glass ones, wooden ones, she even had a stuffed one that croaked when you squeezed its belly. She moved down to Florida a while back. Tampa, I think it was. Or maybe Pensacola. They get terrible hurricanes down there, just awful. The wind blows the roofs right off the houses, I saw it on the news. The newsman had on a very nice raincoat, though. Bright yellow... kind of like Brother Thomas's tie, actually. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, remembering things. No, my memory is perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: What month is it right now?\n\nPatient: What month? Well, it's October. I'm pretty sure it's October. I just flipped the page on my calendar in the kitchen a few days ago. The bank gives them out for free if you ask nice, the teller with the curly hair always saves me one. This year they have pictures of covered bridges. I always liked covered bridges... my uncle Henry lived out near one in Pennsylvania. Or maybe it was Ohio. He raised pigs out there. Big, muddy things, they smell just awful in the summer heat. But he made his own sausage, and let me tell you, you haven't lived until you've had Uncle Henry's breakfast sausage with a little maple syrup poured over it. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. I was just thinking about New Year's, actually. When I was a little girl, my father would bring out the good radio for New Year's Eve. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. They just fly by so fast now.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Just watching the birds, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? Oh, no, I don't think I've gotten lost. I know my way around the neighborhood well enough. I've lived in the same house for... oh, it must be over forty years now. It's the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree drops so many leaves in the autumn, it's a real chore to rake them all up. My grandson used to come over and help me rake them into big piles, and then he'd jump right in! He was such a rambunctious little boy. Always had scraped knees from climbing things he shouldn't. I used to keep those character band-aids in the bathroom just for him. The ones with the little cartoon dogs on them. He wouldn't let me put a plain one on, no sir. It had to be the cartoon dogs. They don't make band-aids like they used to, though. They just don't stick right if you get them even a little bit wet. I was washing dishes the other day—just a few plates, I don't use the dishwasher much anymore since it's just me—and the band-aid came right off in the soapy water. It was a lemon-scented dish soap. I always buy the lemon kind, it smells so clean. But anyway... no, I haven't been getting lost.\n\nDoctor: Do you need someone else to manage your medicines for you?\n\nPatient: Manage my medicines? Oh, well... I don't think I need anyone to do that for me. I have them all lined up on the kitchen counter, right next to the toaster. It's one of those shiny chrome toasters, the kind that pops up with a real loud clank. My sister gave it to me for Christmas a few years back. She always wraps her presents so beautifully, with those big velvet bows that she ties herself. She learned how to make them at a craft store down in Florida when she lived there. Florida is so humid, though, I could never stand the heat. My hair just frizzes up into an absolute mess the minute I step off the plane. I used to use those big pink curlers to try and tame it, the ones you sleep in? Goodness, they were uncomfortable. Like sleeping on a pile of rocks. But yes, I just take my pills myself when I have my morning tea.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything is wrong with your thinking or memory?\n\nPatient: Wrong with my thinking? No, no, I don't think so. My memory is just fine. I was just remembering the other day about the time my cousin came to visit from upstate. She brought this huge basket of apples from an orchard near her house. Honeycrisp, I think they were. They make the best pies, they really do. You have to peel them just right, though, or the texture gets all mushy when you bake it. I have this little paring knife with a wooden handle that I've used for years, fits right in the palm of my hand. The blade is getting a bit dull, I suppose, but I don't know how to sharpen it. My husband used to do all the sharpening out in the garage on his grinding wheel. Sparks would just fly everywhere! It always made me nervous he was going to catch his shirt on fire. He wore those thick flannel shirts all winter long, the plaid ones. Red and black, mostly. They were so warm, but they took forever to dry on the line. I always loved the smell of clothes dried outside in the fresh air, though... much better than the dryer. But anyway, no, my memory is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having more trouble thinking or remembering than you used to?\n\nPatient: More trouble? Oh, I wouldn't say that. I think I remember things just as well as I ever did. Like yesterday, I remembered to put the trash out right on time. The garbage truck always comes so early on Tuesdays, before the sun is even fully up. It makes such an awful racket, clanging those big metal bins against the side of the truck. I don't know how the driver stands the noise all day long. He must wear earplugs, don't you think? Those little foam ones you roll up between your fingers. I tried wearing those once when my neighbor got a new dog that barked all night. It was a little terrier mix, cute as a button but just wouldn't stop yapping at every shadow. I think it was afraid of the dark. Some dogs are like that, you know. They need a little nightlight just like children do. I always kept a nightlight in the hallway when my kids were small. It was shaped like a little seashell, plugged right into the wall. Gave off the softest pink glow... But no, I don't think I'm having any more trouble than usual.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a medical condition affecting your brain or memory?\n\nPatient: A medical condition for my brain? No, no doctor has ever said anything like that to me. I haven't been to the doctor much lately anyway, except when I went to see Dr. Evans for my bad knee a while back. He has this huge aquarium in his waiting room, right against the wall. It’s full of those little blue and red fish... neon tetras, I think they’re called. They just dart around so fast, back and forth, back and forth. I always wonder who cleans that tank. It must take hours to scrub all that green algae off the glass and clean the little pebbles at the bottom. I had a goldfish bowl once when the kids were little, just a small round glass one we won at the county fair. We named him Barnaby. He lived for a really long time for a fair fish, almost two years! We buried him out by the rosebushes when he finally passed on. Those rosebushes grew so tall that next spring, with these beautiful yellow blooms. They smelled just like lemon drops, so sweet and bright. But no, nobody's told me I have any kind of memory condition.\n\nDoctor: Have you started needing help with your finances?\n\nPatient: Finances? Oh, well, I suppose I haven't been keeping track of things quite the same way. The ladies from church have been checking on me a lot more lately, making sure I have what I need. I actually had to step back from leading the choir. And the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I promised to do. It’s a shame, really, because we were working on this beautiful quilt for the charity auction. All these little squares of fabric, donated by people all over town. Mrs. Higgins brought in a whole bag of old calico dresses she didn't wear anymore. They had the sweetest little floral prints on them, tiny pink and yellow daisies. I always loved calico, it reminds me of the aprons my grandmother used to wear when she baked bread. She’d get flour all over the front of it, just covered in white dust. Her bread was the best, though. So crusty on the outside and soft in the middle. We'd eat it warm with butter and that homemade strawberry jam she kept in the cellar. The cellar was always so cool and damp, smelled like earth and old potatoes... but anyway, yes, I've had to stop helping out as much because I just can't keep my promises straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well... no, I don't think I need help with that. I still write the checks out myself every month. I sit down at the kitchen table with my special blue pen. It's one of those gel pens, you know? It writes so smooth, just glides right across the paper. My granddaughter gave it to me for my birthday last year. She’s such a thoughtful girl, always finding little things she thinks I'll like. She just started her first year of high school, can you believe it? Time goes by so fast. It feels like just yesterday she was running around the yard catching fireflies in a glass jar. We used to poke holes in the lid so they could breathe, and she’d keep them on her nightstand until they stopped glowing. Then we'd let them go in the morning. The dew would still be on the grass, getting our slippers all wet. I always wore those fuzzy pink slippers, the ones with the little pom-poms on the toes... but no, I manage my bills just fine.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt sad or low most days?\n\nPatient: Sad or low? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, everybody has their days, but I haven't been feeling down like that. Mostly I just sit on the porch in the afternoons and watch the neighborhood. The Miller boy across the street got a new bicycle, one of those bright red ones with the shiny chrome handlebars. He rides it up and down the sidewalk, ringing the little bell. It reminds me of the bell on the ice cream truck when I was a little girl. We used to hear it from three streets over and go running inside to beg my mother for a nickel. A nickel could get you a whole twin popsicle back then! We’d split it down the middle, me and my brother. He always wanted the cherry side, and I liked the orange. Orange always tasted like sunshine, you know? Like those hot July days when the tar on the road would get all soft and stick to the bottom of your bare feet. My mother would scold us for tracking black marks onto her clean linoleum floor. She kept that floor so spotless you could eat off it... but no, I haven't been feeling sad or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been missing any bill payments lately?\n\nPatient: Missing payments? Oh, well... no, I haven't missed any. I always make sure they go out on time. I walk them down to the blue mailbox on the corner every Tuesday morning. It's a nice little walk, past the Henderson's house. They just planted the most beautiful hydrangeas along their fence. Huge, blue blooms, like giant cotton candy but blue, you know? They remind me of a dress I had when I was a young woman. I wore it to a dance at the VFW hall, it had this full skirt that would just twirl and twirl when you spun around. The band was playing Glenn Miller that night, and the trumpet player had the shiniest shoes I'd ever seen. You could see the lights reflecting right off the toes of them. They must have polished them for hours. My husband used to polish his shoes like that every Sunday before church. He'd lay out a newspaper on the floor so he wouldn't get polish on the rug... but yes, my bills always get mailed right on time.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with cooking meals?\n\nPatient: Cooking meals? Oh, no... I don't need any help with that. I still do all my own cooking. Just the other day I made a big pot of beef stew. I let it simmer on the stove for hours, and the whole kitchen smelled like carrots and thyme. I always use fresh carrots, the ones with the green leafy tops still on them. They remind me of the garden my grandfather used to keep behind his house. He grew tomatoes as big as softballs, I swear. We used to eat them right off the vine, still warm from the sun, with just a little sprinkle of salt from a glass shaker. He kept that salt shaker right in the front pocket of his overalls. Those overalls were so faded, almost white at the knees from where he'd kneel in the dirt all day. He had this old hound dog named Buster that would follow him up and down the rows, just sniffing at the dirt and trying to catch the big fat bumblebees in his mouth... but no, I manage the cooking all by myself just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Drive? Oh, yes, I still drive myself. I take my little Buick out when I need to go to the market. It's a very reliable car, that Buick. The color is a lovely shade of silver, almost like the color of the moon on a really clear, crisp autumn night. You know those nights when the air is so sharp it almost hurts to breathe, but it smells like woodsmoke? My father used to build the most wonderful fires in our fireplace on nights like that. He’d bring in these huge oak logs, and they’d crackle and pop, sending little orange sparks flying up the chimney like tiny fireflies. We used to catch fireflies in glass jars during the summer. We’d punch holes in the metal lids with a nail and a hammer so they could breathe. The hammer had a wooden handle that was all smooth and worn down from years of use... but yes, I do drive myself around just fine.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF03.90, F06.7, F32.9, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, nice to meet you too, doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, I just don’t have the push I used to have. For anything, really. Like my choir... I used to never miss a Sunday. Brother Thomas leads it, and he always wears these real loud yellow ties. I told him once, I said, 'Thomas, that tie is brighter than the morning sun.' He’s a good man, though. His wife makes the best potato salad for the church picnics, she puts that sweet relish in it. I always ask her for the recipe but she won't give it up. \n\nBut anyway... I just don't go to choir now. Or do my quilting. I was working on a nice quilt, but I ran out of the blue thread, and the sewing store downtown closed up a few years back. It used to be a bakery before that, they had these little cherry tarts. So now... most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... usually just the game shows. The commercials are so loud these days, aren't they? Selling all sorts of mops and car insurance. But I just sit there with it on low, even though I know folks are expecting me to come visit or show up to things. I'm not crying into my soup or anything like that. Not like my neighbor Shirley. Now she gets real depressed. She lost her little dog last spring and she just wept and wept. He always made sure to put my magazines inside the screen door so they wouldn't get wet when it rained. It’s been raining so much lately, hasn't it? My sister gave them to me in this little ceramic pot that looked like a frog. She always liked frogs... used to collect them. Big, muddy things, they smell just awful in the summer heat. But he made his own sausage, and let me tell you, you haven't lived until you've had Uncle Henry's breakfast sausage with a little maple syrup poured over it. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. I was just thinking about New Year's, actually. When I was a little girl, my father would bring out the good radio for New Year's Eve. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. They just fly by so fast now.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. I know my way around the neighborhood well enough. I've lived in the same house for... oh, it must be over forty years now. It's the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. He was such a rambunctious little boy. Always had scraped knees from climbing things he shouldn't. I used to keep those character band-aids in the bathroom just for him. They just don't stick right if you get them even a little bit wet. He wore those thick flannel shirts all winter long, the plaid ones. Red and black, mostly. They were so warm, but they took forever to dry on the line. I always loved the smell of clothes dried outside in the fresh air, though... much better than the dryer. I don't know how the driver stands the noise all day long. He must wear earplugs, don't you think? Those little foam ones you roll up between your fingers. I tried wearing those once when my neighbor got a new dog that barked all night. It was a little terrier mix, cute as a button but just wouldn't stop yapping at every shadow. I think it was afraid of the dark. Some dogs are like that, you know. They need a little nightlight just like children do. I always kept a nightlight in the hallway when my kids were small. It was shaped like a little seashell, plugged right into the wall. Gave off the softest pink glow... It must take hours to scrub all that green algae off the glass and clean the little pebbles at the bottom. It’s a shame, really, because we were working on this beautiful quilt for the charity auction. All these little squares of fabric, donated by people all over town. Mrs. Higgins brought in a whole bag of old calico dresses she didn't wear anymore. They had the sweetest little floral prints on them, tiny pink and yellow daisies. I always loved calico, it reminds me of the aprons my grandmother used to wear when she baked bread. She’d get flour all over the front of it, just covered in white dust. Her bread was the best, though. So crusty on the outside and soft in the middle. We'd eat it warm with butter and that homemade strawberry jam she kept in the cellar. Oh, well... no, I don't think I need help with that. I still write the checks out myself every month. I sit down at the kitchen table with my special blue pen. It's one of those gel pens, you know? She just started her first year of high school, can you believe it? Time goes by so fast. It feels like just yesterday she was running around the yard catching fireflies in a glass jar. We used to poke holes in the lid so they could breathe, and she’d keep them on her nightstand until they stopped glowing. Then we'd let them go in the morning. The dew would still be on the grass, getting our slippers all wet. I always wore those fuzzy pink slippers, the ones with the little pom-poms on the toes... but no, I manage my bills just fine.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt sad or low most days?\n\nPatient: Sad or low? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, everybody has their days, but I haven't been feeling down like that. Mostly I just sit on the porch in the afternoons and watch the neighborhood. The Miller boy across the street got a new bicycle, one of those bright red ones with the shiny chrome handlebars. He rides it up and down the sidewalk, ringing the little bell. It reminds me of the bell on the ice cream truck when I was a little girl. We used to hear it from three streets over and go running inside to beg my mother for a nickel. A nickel could get you a whole twin popsicle back then! We’d split it down the middle, me and my brother. He always wanted the cherry side, and I liked the orange. Orange always tasted like sunshine, you know? Like those hot July days when the tar on the road would get all soft and stick to the bottom of your bare feet. My mother would scold us for tracking black marks onto her clean linoleum floor. She kept that floor so spotless you could eat off it... but no, I haven't been feeling sad or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been missing any bill payments lately?\n\nPatient: Missing payments? Oh, well... no, I haven't missed any. I always make sure they go out on time. I walk them down to the blue mailbox on the corner every Tuesday morning. It's a nice little walk, past the Henderson's house. They just planted the most beautiful hydrangeas along their fence. Huge, blue blooms, like giant cotton candy but blue, you know? They remind me of a dress I had when I was a young woman. I wore it to a dance at the VFW hall, it had this full skirt that would just twirl and twirl when you spun around. The band was playing Glenn Miller that night, and the trumpet player had the shiniest shoes I'd ever seen. You could see the lights reflecting right off the toes of them. They must have polished them for hours. My husband used to polish his shoes like that every Sunday before church. He'd lay out a newspaper on the floor so he wouldn't get polish on the rug... but yes, my bills always get mailed right on time.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with cooking meals?\n\nPatient: Cooking meals? Oh, no... I don't need any help with that. I still do all my own cooking. Just the other day I made a big pot of beef stew. I let it simmer on the stove for hours, and the whole kitchen smelled like carrots and thyme. I always use fresh carrots, the ones with the green leafy tops still on them. They remind me of the garden my grandfather used to keep behind his house. He grew tomatoes as big as softballs, I swear. We used to eat them right off the vine, still warm from the sun, with just a little sprinkle of salt from a glass shaker. He kept that salt shaker right in the front pocket of his overalls. Those overalls were so faded, almost white at the knees from where he'd kneel in the dirt all day. He had this old hound dog named Buster that would follow him up and down the rows, just sniffing at the dirt and trying to catch the big fat bumblebees in his mouth... but no, I manage the cooking all by myself just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you still drive by yourself?\n\nPatient: Drive? Oh, yes, I still drive myself. I take my little Buick out when I need to go to the market. It's a very reliable car, that Buick. The color is a lovely shade of silver, almost like the color of the moon on a really clear, crisp autumn night. You know those nights when the air is so sharp it almost hurts to breathe, but it smells like woodsmoke? My father used to build the most wonderful fires in our fireplace on nights like that. He’d bring in these huge oak logs, and they’d crackle and pop, sending little orange sparks flying up the chimney like tiny fireflies. We used to catch fireflies in glass jars during the summer. We’d punch holes in the metal lids with a nail and a hammer so they could breathe. The hammer had a wooden handle that was all smooth and worn down from years of use... but yes, I do drive myself around just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

The dew would still be on the grass, getting our slippers all wet. Those overalls were so faded, almost white at the knees from where he'd kneel in the dirt all day. He had this old hound dog named Buster that would follow him up and down the rows, just sniffing at the dirt and trying to catch the big fat bumblebees in his mouth... but no, I manage the cooking all by myself just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you still drive by yourself?\n\nPatient: Drive? We’d punch holes in the metal lids with a nail and a hammer so they could breathe. Handling my finances? No, no, I still do all that myself. Nobody else touches my checkbook. I keep it in the top drawer of my desk, right next to the stationary. It's this beautiful heavy paper, cream-colored, with my initials embossed at the top in gold ink. I bought it at a little stationery shop downtown years ago. The shop owner was this sweet man who wore a bowtie every single day. He had a whole collection of them, polka dots, stripes, little paisley patterns. He told me once he bought them all on a trip to London. Oh, I've always wanted to go to London and see the changing of the guard. The soldiers in those tall, fuzzy black hats... they must get so dreadfully hot in the summertime. I wonder what they're made of? Bear fur, maybe? That seems so heavy to wear on your head all day... but anyway, no, I take care of all my own money. Nobody else handles it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF03.90, F06.7, F32.9, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, nice to meet you too, doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, I just don’t have the push I used to have. For anything, really. Like my choir... I used to never miss a Sunday. Brother Thomas leads it, and he always wears these real loud yellow ties. I told him once, I said, 'Thomas, that tie is brighter than the morning sun.' He’s a good man, though. His wife makes the best potato salad for the church picnics, she puts that sweet relish in it. I always ask her for the recipe but she won't give it up. \n\nBut anyway... I just don't go to choir now. Or do my quilting. I was working on a nice quilt, but I ran out of the blue thread, and the sewing store downtown closed up a few years back. It used to be a bakery before that, they had these little cherry tarts. So now... most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... usually just the game shows. Big, muddy things, they smell just awful in the summer heat. Real maple syrup, not that fake stuff in the plastic bottles they sell now. The real stuff comes in glass, sometimes shaped like a little log cabin. My sister had a cabin up by the lake, actually... but yes, I know what month it is.\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: The year? Well, they do tend to blend together a bit these days. It was one of those big wooden ones that sat right in the parlor, with the glowing glass tubes in the back. I used to just sit and watch them glow, they got so warm. Our cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby—he loved to sleep right behind that radio to soak up the heat. He was a wonderful mouser, that cat. He caught a mouse right in our pantry once, right next to the flour bin. We had to keep the flour in a big metal tin back then, otherwise the weevils would get into it. Have you ever seen a weevil? Just nasty little bugs. They'll ruin a whole batch of biscuits if you aren't paying attention. My mother made the best biscuits, though. She used a whole lot of buttermilk. We'd get the buttermilk from the milkman, he had a little truck that rattled down the dirt road... but what was it? Oh, the year. Honestly, I'd probably have to look at the top of the newspaper to tell you the exact number. They just fly by so fast now.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Where we are? Well, we're in this room right now. It's a nice enough room, I suppose. The chairs are comfortable. Not like the chairs at the dentist's office. Oh, those are just dreadful, aren't they? They lean you so far back you feel like all the blood is rushing right to your head. And that bright light shining right in your eyes... it always gives me a headache. I used to take aspirin for headaches, but then my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she told me that peppermint tea is much better for that sort of thing. She grows her own peppermint in little window boxes. They smell wonderful in the summer. She gave me a cutting once, but I think I watered it too much. I'm usually so good with plants, you know, with my African violets and all, but herbs can be fussy. Some of them like the soil dry, some like it damp... it's a lot to keep track of. But, um... what did you ask me? Oh, right, where we are. I know it's a doctor's office of some sort, but I couldn't tell you the exact name of the building or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed more help lately with paying your bills?\n\nPatient: Paying my bills? Oh, well, I suppose I manage alright with the mail, though I have had to step back a bit from things lately. I used to be the one leading the church choir, you know. Every Sunday, standing right up there in the front. I had this beautiful navy blue dress I wore for the big performances, the one with the little pearl buttons down the sleeves. I bought it at a department store downtown... oh, what was the name of it? They had the most wonderful perfume counter right when you walked in. It always smelled like roses and powder. My mother loved rose water, she used to dab it on her wrists before church. She had a little glass bottle with a silver stopper. Anyway, I had to stop doing the choir, and I stopped helping out with the projects at the senior center, too. I just couldn't seem to keep up with what I'd promised to do, and I'd forget the sheet music or the meeting times. Now my friends from church, they come by and check on me a lot more than they used to. They bring casseroles sometimes. Martha brought a tuna noodle one last week, but she uses those crushed potato chips on top instead of breadcrumbs. I always think breadcrumbs are much better, especially if you toast them in a little butter first with some parsley... but yes, I've had to take a step back from my activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most days?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? I don't think so, really. I mean, some days are quieter than others, I suppose. I like to sit by the window when it's quiet. I have a bird feeder right out there, a nice wooden one shaped like a little barn. I put out black oil sunflower seeds because the cardinals just love those. They're such beautiful birds, aren't they? That bright red against the snow in the winter, it's just lovely. My husband, God rest his soul, he built that bird feeder for me. He was always tinkering in the garage with wood. He made a beautiful cedar chest for our bedroom, too. It still smells like cedar when you open it up, even after all these years. I keep my winter quilts in there. You have to keep them packed away properly or the moths will get them. Moths can just ruin a good wool blanket in no time at all. My grandmother used to put mothballs in everything, but oh, that smell is just awful. It gets right into the fabric and you can never wash it out... but no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. I know my way around the neighborhood well enough. I've lived in the same house for... oh, it must be over forty years now. It's the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree drops so many leaves in the autumn, it's a real chore to rake them all up. My grandson used to come over and help me rake them into big piles, and then he'd jump right in! He was such a rambunctious little boy. Always had scraped knees from climbing things he shouldn't. I used to keep those character band-aids in the bathroom just for him. The ones with the little cartoon dogs on them. He wouldn't let me put a plain one on, no sir. It had to be the cartoon dogs. They don't make band-aids like they used to, though. They just don't stick right if you get them even a little bit wet. I think I remember things just as well as I ever did. Like yesterday, I remembered to put the trash out right on time. The garbage truck always comes so early on Tuesdays, before the sun is even fully up. It makes such an awful racket, clanging those big metal bins against the side of the truck. I don't know how the driver stands the noise all day long. He must wear earplugs, don't you think? Those little foam ones you roll up between your fingers. I tried wearing those once when my neighbor got a new dog that barked all night. It was a little terrier mix, cute as a button but just wouldn't stop yapping at every shadow. No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, everybody has their days, but I haven't been feeling down like that. Mostly I just sit on the porch in the afternoons and watch the neighborhood. The Miller boy across the street got a new bicycle, one of those bright red ones with the shiny chrome handlebars. He rides it up and down the sidewalk, ringing the little bell. It reminds me of the bell on the ice cream truck when I was a little girl. We used to hear it from three streets over and go running inside to beg my mother for a nickel. A nickel could get you a whole twin popsicle back then! We’d split it down the middle, me and my brother. He always wanted the cherry side, and I liked the orange. Orange always tasted like sunshine, you know? Like those hot July days when the tar on the road would get all soft and stick to the bottom of your bare feet. She kept that floor so spotless you could eat off it... but no, I haven't been feeling sad or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been missing any bill payments lately?\n\nPatient: Missing payments? Oh, well... no, I haven't missed any. I always make sure they go out on time. I walk them down to the blue mailbox on the corner every Tuesday morning. It's a nice little walk, past the Henderson's house. They just planted the most beautiful hydrangeas along their fence. Huge, blue blooms, like giant cotton candy but blue, you know?
